ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-12.ana.ud2204e143	The film 'Made in Dagenham' is now going to cinema. It's about how the first equal pay law in the United Kingdom got in place. The Ford factory in Dagenham, London, was the largest car factory in Europe in 1968, with about 55 000 male employees and 187 female employees, who sewed the trains to the car seats. Women's wages are the same as unskilled workers. But, as the heroine asks, is it because the workers are unskilled, or is it because they are women? The women take up the fight, strike, get the factory closed, travel around the country to get women at other car factories to strike. In a desperate moment, our heroine calls out to his husband: what do you think this is really about? We know what this is all about. The fight for equal pay is at the heart of what is women's liberation and the gender equality struggle. It is both about the economic autonomy of women and about the value of women's work being assessed and praised as much as the value of men's work. The contribution of women to the welfare state and to the welfare society is significant. Now they're going to have to earn what they deserve. In the last period, the government dropped the Equal pay Commission, which gave a broad review of the pay gap in Norway. The hourly wage gap between the average male and female earnings in Norway is 15pst. In addition, we know that the income gap between women and men is much larger, as men have capital income, work more and reach higher in the job hierarchy. The Commission also shows that the fight for equal pay must take place in many policy areas: employment policy, employer policy, gender equality policy and family policy. A sex-shared labour market, a lot of part-time work and the gender-shared division of work of home and care work must take the main blame for the pay gap between women and men in Norway. The message we are handling today is a follow-up to the Equal pay Commission. In the message we do the following grip: We will ensure gender-related wage statistics at the level of activity and the employer's obligation to disclose wages and salaries in the event of suspicion of discrimination. We also need to clarify the rights that parents have under and after parental leave, in order to prevent aggravating employment conditions and weaker wage developments. It is not rare to come back from leave and have had work tasks other than those you had before the leave. We facilitate a more equal parenthood through a clearer third of parental leave. The possibility for women to participate in the labour market has been made possible through a generous parental leave and full childcare coverage. Nevertheless, when we have children, we have a weaker wage trend for women. It's probably because there are still women who take primary responsibility for the children. With the third, we want to strengthen the spring quota, let the free quota be discussed and that equal distribution of care work should begin when the child is small, and continue as they grow. So the goal is for a more equal distribution of care work at home to be sustainable. We also propose to give women the right to one hour of breast-feeding every day until the child is one year old. It gives an incentive for mothers to go back to work earlier, and it may in turn affect the withdrawal of the leave. We take important steps in this message, but there are still some that remain. I'll especially bring out the part-time question. Soria Moria II has stated that the government will assess legislative changes to strengthen the right to full-time. Two of the government parties at their national meetings have adopted offensive policies in this field. I would like to reject the fact that all the proposals that the right-hand side promotes to weaken trade union movement, to weaken job protection, to weaken working time regulations and to postpone competition are strategies for equal pay. On the contrary, we see that the right-hand side's policy is increasing the pay gap. It's with the Swedish government we've been moving in this field, which has been a topic at least since the women's strikes in London in 1968. In Norway, this has been a political issue since women raised it at the LO Congress over 100 years ago. Like the girls in Dagenham, we celebrate today taking a big step in the right direction, but we are impatient to take the next one.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-12.ana.ud2204e219	Throughout generations, women have been accepted to be cheaper workers than men, whether they have worked in industry, worked as servants, as modern civil servants, or today as nurses. In the past, this was defended by the fact that women were cared for. Their income was in addition to that of their spouse. Yes, until the 1970s, we experienced that in the case of high unemployment, women were defended first, because they were taken care of. Such attitudes are not accepted in a modern equalized society in 2011, but the problem still exists in full, rightly in a different disguise and with a different reason than before. In 1947, the pay gap between women and men who had the same job was between 20 and 40 pst. The reason was family relations and the burden of living. Fortunately, the type of equal pay discrimination exists to a very small extent today and is also prohibited by the Equality Act. But we don't have equal pay in Norway. We have roughly equal pay for equal work, but it does not remove the fact that women earn about 85 pst. of what is men's hourly wages. If you look at the total income, that distance is even larger. Female teachers and male teachers serve pretty much the same. The same thing happens to male and female physiotherapists. But in women-dominated professions, you earn less than in men-dominated professions with equal education. This is supported by the fact that women choose low-wage workers as nurses and pre-school teachers, while men choose masculine professions as engineers and economists. Of course, an engineer with three years of high school education should have paid well; he's working on important things like constructing roads and calculating how much reinforcement iron is needed in the new health home. Of course, we want the market mechanism to ensure that we get the best engineers, but what about preschool teachers and nurses? They also have three years of higher education. They're working on such important things as taking care of our elderly and educating our kids. Here, too, we must have the best. What makes us still value men and their professions higher than women? I am pleased that we have a government that will now do something about the lack of equal pay. But this is not solved by the fact that market forces are controlled. So there will be bombings when right-wing parties imagine that privatisation and outsourcing of care contribute to equal pay, as they are analysing the current situation. Didn't they bring the Adecco case? The speech of the figures is very clear: in the Upsaling Home that is open to competition, it is the case that the women's employees have a lower pension, there are fewer employees and poorer working conditions. In Bogane's nursing home in Stavanger, which has been run by Norlandia since 2002, there are as many complaints as there are in the rest of the 16 nursing homes in Stavanger. At Ammerudlunden, which was run by Adecco Health, they are not paid overtime. If you review the private care institutions versus the public, you will see that there are on average lower wages, lower pensions, lower overtime pay and lower sick pay. Ergo does not contribute to a competitive outlay of women in the care professions to equal pay, it contributes to a lower wage for women. In addition, the Right provides for more flexibility – read: weakening the Working Environment Act. Many women are already treated as second-class workers in the Norwegian labour market. In the healthcare sector, part-time is combined with additional shifts. The hotel and restaurant industries have 0 positions and ring guards. The working conditions are deemed to be completed after the work on the contracted guards has been carried out. In practice, it means zero notice period. Moreover, when the right-hand side will weaken the protection against dismissal and expose the care sector to competition, this does not contribute to more equal pay but to a more gender-sensitive action with less equal pay. In addition, when the right side wants to weaken the role of fathers and weaken the father's quota, it does not help us to get any closer to equal pay. Rather, it contributes to the opposite, because we see that when women have children, they are weakened in relation to their pay terms. Men who have children are strengthened according to their pay terms. What we're looking at is that all the shortcomings in measures proposed by the right-hand side help us to move away from equal pay, as opposed to the government, which has proposed many good measures.

Then we take care of the child’s need for time with both mother and father, while ensuring freedom of choice. But we're still not in the goal. But at the end of the day, people have to take responsibility for their own everyday lives and their own future. 30–40 years ago, the fight against the last generation of kindergartens, parental leave schemes and other arrangements for women to be able to get into work. This has resulted in Norway having both high levels of professional participation among women and the fact that we still have many children. That's why we have to be careful now, so that we avoid a situation where welfare schemes eventually become an obstacle to achieving gender equality.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-12.ana.ud2204e607	I am proud that the government has presented the first big-time report on equal pay. It shows how gender equality policies can help reduce the pay gap between women and men. In the Communication, the government analyses and describes the problem of equal pay. The Communication sets out clear objectives and contains concrete measures that will strengthen equality between Norwegian families and Norwegian working life. An egalitarian society is a prerequisite for gender pay balance. In the Communication, we show that family and gender policies have an impact on pay differentials and on economic cohesion between the sexes. Discrimination and discrimination, which are rooted in traditions and narrow gender roles, can and must be met with political measures and laws that give more freedom to all – and better financial distribution. Women still serve on average less than men per hour of work. And women have significantly lower annual income than men. The Equal Pay Commission gave a thorough analysis of the situation. Our announcement is based on this knowledge, on the assessments and on the hearing opinions. The pay gap is not covered by one measure alone. We need action both in terms of wage formation, in terms of family policy and in terms of working life to achieve our objectives. There is a need for solutions in wage and salary statements, which may be altered by political decisions. Therefore, in the Communication, we present proposals in areas where authorities can and should take responsibility. The main message to Kadra was that Somali women differed according to Norwegian law, but lived on with the men they were married to under Islamic law. The number of lone dependants is two and a half times higher among Somalis than among other immigrants and the average for the whole population. I then ask myself, what is the reason why people who come from a conservative Sunni Muslim country suddenly choose to differ in Norway? Because we're talking about resource-powerful women who've managed to escape from war on Africa's horns. They have all been subjected to great trials and risked a lot on the journey. The goal is a life in Norway where they can live in freedom – and take care of their family. But well installed in Norway, they meet a social society that indirectly encourages couples to stand out on paper and promote single mothers with large children’s flocks. This has tragic consequences for many families. Because the fictitious divorce is not just a paper fraud. In fear of having unannounced visits from NAV, the man often leaves home during the day, and many Somali men apply to the khat environment in Greenland.’ The consequences of what Kadra writes give me new concerns – concerns that we should all have been concerned about, namely the fact that children grow up in dissolved homes without a fixed framework, which in turn creates chaos in the home. I think we politicians must dare to see the truth in their eyes: the Norwegian welfare system is being abused for economic reasons, where unfortunately the immigrant children become the big losers. Human Rights Service has reported in several rounds on identity-related, fictitious asylum seekers, child abuse for residence, tricking with the number of children’s identity in order to obtain more support and lies about marital status. Let's do two examples: an immigrant woman who tells the truth. She's married, but she can't live with the granddaddy, because the granddaddy's still on the run abroad. She's alone with two kids aged four and five, participating in an introduction program. She will receive a total of NOK 169 052 in child benefit and in introduction benefit. Let's take the second example: a immigrant woman doesn't tell the truth. She's married, lives with the granddaddy, but she tells me she's divorced. They also have two children aged four and five, and she participates in an introduction programme. She will be paid a total of 332,000 kroner in transitional social assistance, introductory social assistance, child welfare and support for child care. In these two examples, the answer gives itself. Why in the world are you going to miss thousands of dollars by telling me you're married? ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-12.ana.ud2204e1052	Before I go over to talk about how we can contribute to the employment of as many people as possible with a migrant background, and how to ensure that we live on social security as little as possible, I would like to say something in general about the abuse of social security schemes. The State of work and welfare carries out risk analyses to identify areas of social benefit with a special risk of mispayments and abuse. My ministry has been given an assessment of the functioning of the social welfare scheme for lone dependants for recipients with a migrant background. That report concludes that the social welfare scheme works well on the way, with a view to helping to get the beneficiaries with a migrant background into work. However, the scheme does not work for everyone. It is the most resource-intensive receivers that have the best results. But if we compare the Norwegian Somalis as a group with other groups, we see that people with Somali background in Norway have low employment and high unemployment. Very many in this group have government transfers as their principal income. Many Somalis are in great need of qualification, including: due to a small school background and short stay in Norway. They come from a warring country, where the education system has been destroyed for a long time. In recent years, Somalis have made up a relatively large group of newcomers to Norway. About a third of those who are here have lived here for four years or less. The introduction programme is therefore a central instrument for providing these groups with basic qualifications. In 2009, Somalia was the largest single group in the programme. The aim is to strengthen the opportunities for newcomers to participate in professional and social life and to strengthen their financial autonomy. Women in the introduction programme have a consistently lower transition to work than men, particularly women from Afghanistan, Iraq and Somalia. In a recent report, the programme appears to be running and functioning differently for women and men from these groups. The report provides knowledge of possible reasons why some groups of women are making it worse in the introduction programme. It is important for me to stress that we should not lower the level of ambition for women in the introduction programme, nor for women with Somali background. These include: therefore, the Grand Duchy is now considering a proposal to strengthen the State supervision of the implementation of the introduction programme in the municipalities. Women with low education and a high level of care also need to receive individually adapted and high-quality induction programmes, and it must be a clear objective for women to get involved in work or education. It is essential to ensure equality and economic autonomy for these women, but also to balance social differences and combat child poverty in Norway. Schemes such as the New Chance Qualification Programme are important instruments for reaching people who are out of work after living for a long time in Norway. Figures from the Directorate of Labour and Welfare show that 40 pst. by the participants in the qualification programme are immigrants, of which the largest group from Somalia. New opportunities, which are thus targeted at women who have not lived on transfers but who have been at home and out of work for a long time, have been focusing on women with a background from particularly low-employment rural groups since 2009. somaliere. For some immigrants, the meeting with the Norwegian welfare state can be a challenge and the need for information can be great. I believe it is important that everyone in Norway has a basic knowledge of the Norwegian welfare community – how it works, how it is built up, why it works, what values it is built on – with both rights and obligations that belong to it. That is why I have commissioned Vox, the national expert body for competence policy, to develop a separate training scheme for newly arrived immigrants. For workers in the State of Employment and Welfare it may be difficult to communicate what the State of Welfare implies in a clear way to persons with an experience background different from that of the Norwegian. This is why a competence development measure has been launched for Nav staff. Then we must not forget that it is not just that people with Somali background are struggling on the Norwegian labour market because they lack qualification. Many well-trained Norwegian Somalis also struggle to get a job. Therefore, systematic recruitment is important in order to take advantage of the wide range of competences in the population, including those with a Somali background, and the public sector interview system is an important measure in this context. It requires that at least one applicant with a migrant background be invited for an interview if he or she is qualified for the job. This scheme is already in place in the country, with good results. It is now introduced as a voluntary scheme in the municipalities, and I hope that the municipalities also have positive experience with this. Oslo Municipality, which is part of the Progress Party, is one of the municipalities that has good results, but I looked to my disappointment that the Progress Party strongly criticized the voluntary scheme – which we are now launching for other municipalities – last week. The representative Eriksson asks me if I am willing to tackle the problem from a cultural point of view. First of all, I'm concerned with results. I am committed to improving the integration policy instruments we have. It means, of course, that when a problem is overrepresented in a particular ethnic group, it is relevant and important information that can help us to solve it. But we have to draw a sharp distinction between that and pursuing an integration policy that makes it seem that certain social problems are culturally dependent. There has not been a lack of social security in Norwegian society until the Somalis arrived, and there are very many Norwegian Somalis who are making a great effort for this society, both in the working and social life. This is why the government's integration policy is about language, work and equality and not about cultural struggles, and that is an important distinction in integration policy. In summary, I would like to underline the following: Social security fraud is unacceptable, regardless of who is behind it. Systematic work is required to detect abuse. The hub control and collection do this. We need to do what we can to help several Norwegian Somalis get into work, by improving the existing instruments, and we need to facilitate that well-qualified people, including Norwegian Somalis, get jobs for which they are qualified.

After taking a divorce abroad, it turns out that you can't trust the Folkeregister in Norway blindly. Are you willing to look at the possibilities of providing other evidence of that divorce in order to be entitled to the right arrangements? That's exactly what's at the heart of the problem. It is precisely this that is also at the heart of what the Norwegian Somali environment points to, at least when they are in contact with them.

There will be a need to lease in such capacity for up to 40 days, and this will be a new but necessary cost. It is therefore proposed that the Stortinget grant 14 million. to cover those costs and to hire a civil operator. Against this background, it is proposed to raise the cap. 455 Rescue, item 1 Operating expenses, with 14 million. kr in 2011.

Greater demands are made on municipalities to organise their services for individual solutions according to the basic needs of the individual.» This is a development Christian People's Party wants. It is essential that municipalities organise their services better and build up knowledge of how to fulfil their rights. There is also a need for measures to enable vulnerable groups to be assisted in the complaint of a breach of their rights, including: by making patient and user representatives more accessible. The Christian People's Party has, among other things: asked the Minister of Health and Health if she would establish a free telephone service for the elderly who are experiencing a breach of the guarantee of dignity – a telephone number of the individual himself or family member may call to help with complaints about decisions or lack of services. The Minister of Health and Welfare does not want to introduce such an available service, and it is disappointing. The representative, Eriksson, made a very good contribution. Similarly, looking up how parents fight to get the rights they need has frustrated me and the Christian People's Party. In the case of cases, for example: care allowance or care allowance, where one has seen that this government has made it much more difficult for parents who want to take care of their own children, it upsets me to hear the stories about how their everyday life is. One example: a mother who is a physiotherapist, had one wish, and it was to be able to be at home with the child and train it, so that the child could have the most active life possible. She did not receive a care allowance – she did not receive support to be at home. The result was that she had to go to work, and a lot of people were hired to do the same job, to give the same training that the little boy could have got from just the mother. So it became much more expensive for society, and it became much worse for the family that wanted to be home to improve the everyday life of the little boy. This is one example, and there are many examples. Therefore, I just want to say that it is great that TV 2 has been running such a long campaign where they have revealed the case on a case-by-case basis so that we can just deal with a matter like this and ensure that the people who have legal rights, but who do not get them, get them. Therefore, the Christian People's Party also has great expectations of what has been said both yesterday and today, that it should be followed up by the government. The Christian People's Party agrees that it is necessary to consider whether sanctions should be given to the county and to the health authorities, as the majority of them write in the comments. ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-12.ana.ud2204e1516	First of all, I would like to say that this is a very important debate, and there is reason to thank both proposers and other activists, including the media, who have contributed to putting it on the agenda. In addition to the concrete proposal, I would say that this goes straight into what will be at the heart of many of the welfare challenges and the political challenges we face. And, not least, as a follow-up to much of the work behind the power investigation, this debate is very much about how we organise the welfare and the issue of the relationship between lawmaking and politics. The Norwegian welfare society is characterised by the establishment of a number of mechanisms whereby citizens have access to community benefits or services. Many of these schemes are established by law or law and some are designed to give citizens legal rights to the State or municipality. There are sliding transitions both in terms of the content of the rights and whether it is actually a right, in my opinion. At the same time, the welfare society involves obligations for citizens, including: that those who are capable of doing so must contribute to the financing of the fissile layer of the welfare state. The scope of statutory welfare rights and the way in which rights are designed in the law varies between different administrative areas, which, in my view, makes it quite inappropriate to have a comprehensive analysis of this field. In some cases, the law gives precisely defined rights. Other rights provisions are designed in such a way that the level of benefits depends on discretionary considerations, often left to the management's so-called free judgement. In such cases, however, citizens may be required to comply with a minimum standard of performance. Therefore, it is difficult to compare one right to another right. The establishment of welfare goods and questions of how to design a right offer complex policy assessments. It is often easy to point out good arguments for public service to citizens in a given situation. At the same time, it must be borne in mind that the establishment of rights involves the allocation of limited resources — yes, those not covered by rights will have to be weaker than those actually protected by a right. A too detailed regulation of citizens' welfare rights will limit the political and administrative possibilities to make the necessary priorities, and I also note that most speakers before me have been involved. This is, and must still be, an overall political responsibility which must lie with central and local elected bodies. The legalisation of detailed welfare rights may contribute to the redefinition of issues of distribution which are initially to be answered by policy considerations into questions of legal rights, which may in turn be brought before the courts. In addition, such legal proceedings are binding on the budget freedom of the Grand Duchy. It also connects users who do not have the money, do not have the skills or do not want to go to the courts with their case. In my view, it would not be appropriate to have a common case and a joint inquiry, as the proposal for a representative is based on. Rights control must be achieved through the general work of the part of the administration working on it, where there is also a lot of power, and the quality assurance arrangements that exist. A common case will not necessarily strengthen the arrangements, but could, rather, involve a resource-intensive mix of equal and unequal, for which there will be considerable uncertainty about the value of the benefits. This does not mean that one does not agree that one should have good control and good opportunity to see to what extent one complies with the rights regimes. In my view, it has to be from sector to sector and from one level of society to another level of society that had to exercise these rights. Everyone shall be able to participate in the welfare society. A strong public sector is a prerequisite for balancing and equivalent conditions for all. In its policy platform, the government has been clearly committed to improving, strengthening and renewing public welfare systems. Only robust public solutions can over time be an alternative to the privatisation of well-being. The key mechanisms to ensure that citizens receive what they are to do are a competent and ethically conscious administration, good internal control, access to review procedures, other supervisory arrangements, the function of the Ombudsman and access to justice. Of course, we listen and read what the Committee now writes about what they want, towards an assessment of possible sanctions. I would like to take that up with the respective state councils in the fields mentioned, as well as in other relevant fields, in order to assess this in addition to the sanctions that had already been imposed in the law.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-12.ana.ud2204e1736	On 17 November last year, the Minister of Justice presented his statement to the Stortinge on the issue of the possible illegal surveillance of Norwegian citizens under the auspices of an observation group associated with the US Embassy in Oslo. The Minister of Justice stated in the statement that neither he nor his two predecessors had been aware that illegalities had been committed, and that the same was true of the Minister of Foreign Affairs and other members of the Government. The Minister of Justice also pointed to the national attorney’s decision to investigate the case and that the prosecution authority will assess whether there has been a criminal offence. In its work on this matter, the Judicial Committee has been particularly concerned with two aspects. Firstly, the description of the factual facts relating to the possible illegal monitoring of Norwegian citizens is indeed correct. Secondly, the Committee has been concerned about whether the seven measures outlined by the Minister of Justice in the statement are sufficient to ensure legal certainty, good public administration and, at the same time, the security of embassies and foreign representation in Norway. The Committee stresses that there is a ban on the exercise of foreign powers in Norwegian territory without Norwegian consent and that there is a requirement for compliance with Norwegian law in Norwegian territory. The activities of the embassies are governed by the Vienna Convention of 18 April 1961 on diplomatic relations and international customary law. A very central aspect of the Committee's work has been the clarification of the extent to which an embassy's own observation measures can be implemented outside its territory in order to ensure its own safety. This was referred to by the Council of State in its statement, and in addition the Committee has asked for clarification in writing to the Minister of Justice. The Committee notes that the Minister of Justice has applied international law as described in that Vienna Convention and that to some extent it provides for safety-related measures in the immediate vicinity of embassies. The Committee points out that it would be in the interest of both the embassies and the Norwegian authorities to clarify this borderline more specifically than is the case today. The Committee considers that the concept of immediate proximity must be interpreted literally and that the general rule is that this grey zone is limited to an absolute minimum. At the same time, the Committee considers that there should be room for some discretion on this issue. The current threat situation and the location of the embassy in question are factors that could be significant. The Committee assumes that security measures outside the border of ownership of embassies, including observation activities, shall be formally notified to the Ministry of Foreign Affairs in advance. The Committee also emphasises the circular note from the Ministry of Foreign Affairs to the diplomatic representations in Oslo, dated 14 January 2011. In the opinion of the Committee, this note makes clear the procedures, responsibilities and interactions between the Ministry of Foreign Affairs and the embassies and representations of each country in a good way on security issues. The Committee also considers it appropriate to comment on a letter from the US Embassy dated as early as 5 May 2000 to the POT – current PST – and the Oslo Police District. This letter clearly describes the establishment of an observation group. But the letter is not registered as received by any of the addressees, nor has it been filed, according to the PST and Oslo police districts. The Committee wonders whether this is the case. The Committee would highlight the transparency on the basis of which the Minister of Justice presented the case. This has been positive for both the information on the matter and for the consideration of the Committee. It is relatively rare that a report is dealt with by a professional committee in retrospect. In the cases that have occurred, the recommendation is that the statement be annexed to the minutes. However, in this case, a unanimous committee establishes that the Stortinget asks the Government to provide appropriate information on the measures it has taken to follow up the Minister of Justice’s presentation. This is due to the nature of the case, and it is underlined by the Committee that the case identifies a need to clarify the lines of responsibility and information in the departments concerned. This is confirmed in the next case on the file, where the Control and Constitutional Committee finds it to criticise the PST for the lack of reporting on this case. A Joint Committee on Foreign Affairs and Defence aligns itself with the position of the Judicial Committee. Finally, I would like to thank the Committee for its good cooperation in this comprehensive and important matter.

As a result of TV 2's revelations, Minister of Justice Knut Storberget made his own statement to the Stortinget about the matter. The Minister of Justice’s statement has been discussed by the Justice Committee – and the Stortinget at the moment, in the previous case – and will therefore not be further affected in my speech. However, the Stockholm Agency for the Secret Services, the EOS Committee, carried out a limited survey as a result of the information provided in the media. The investigation was limited to the role of the Police Security Service in the case. In the light of the findings made during their studies, the EOS Committee chose to send a specific message to the Stortinge. On a general basis, the Committee has stated that it is positive that the EOS Committee makes use of the possibility for them to send such a specific communication to the Stortinget, and also expresses the wish that this should be done in future in such cases in order to ensure timely political discussion in the Stortinget. The Committee has also supported the understanding of its own mandate by the EOS Committee to include monitoring and investigations related to the existence of foreign intelligence activities on Norwegian soil with which the Norwegian security service has also authorised or been known. This also includes, of course, the possibility for the sample to examine how the Norwegian services have managed the situation and to inform the overall authority. According to the instructions of Section 17 of the Police Security Service, the Ministry of Justice is required to report all matters of importance. The EOS Committee has come to the conclusion that the PST broke the instructions at this point when they failed to report to the Ministry of Justice on the service’s knowledge of the SDU’s activities. This is, of course, a serious situation, because the ministry is deprived of the possibility of implementing corrective measures or of setting up Norwegian action and dialogue in line with what the ministry believes to be right. The lack of reporting therefore leads to the kind of situation that we experienced in this case, where it was sometimes tried to shift responsibility in different directions. For its part, the Committee stresses that ‘the reporting obligation is fundamental’ and that the reporting threshold should be low also in the case of allied countries. For my own part, I would like to add that it does not exactly strengthen the PST case when the incumbent head of the PST in the media expresses that she is not really so concerned about focusing on the activities of allied countries in Norway. The Committee stresses in its opinion that: ‘The legal security barriers adopted by the Stortinget are of fundamental importance for citizens’ security against breaches of their powers and that the PST has a specific responsibility to safeguard the same interests’. The EOS Committee also criticises the PST for failing to inform the sample of its knowledge of the activities of the SDU. The Committee has been very clear that, in order to ensure the possibility for the EOS Committee to exercise effective control, it is important that the selection should receive information from the PST on matters of importance. There appears to be no doubt that the SDU business was a matter of importance, and that there should therefore have been both reported to the Ministry and informed to the sample. The Committee considers that this has not happened to be «serious and critical». Those who know the language theme of the Control and Constitutional Committee understand the scope of this opinion. For the Progress Party, I would also like to add that it is always the responsibility of the Council of State to ensure that the service is aware of the threshold set to ensure adequate information for the Ministry. The fact that the service has assessed this error has led, as we heard from the Council of State say in the previous case, to a review of what threshold the Council of State wants to be the basis for future reporting by the services. That's good. With this a unanimous committee position is put before the Stortinget. It is therefore unanimous on all points.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-12.ana.ud2204e2035	Where the majority of the Committee, which is all but the proposer, rejects the proposed representative, it is primarily because the proposers in the document ask the Government to set up a Constitutional Commission to review the Constitution. It is such that the Storting does not normally ask the government to investigate changes to the Constitution. In those cases where the Stortinge has seen it necessary to carry out a more detailed investigation prior to the promotion of constitutional proposals, the investigation committee has been shut down by the Stortinge itself. That being said, the question of the shape and content of the Constitution is an important debate, and we are discussing at each time a number of concrete constitutional proposals presented by members of the Stortinget. So the development of the Constitution is a result of changing political circumstances and political grips. And then a lot of paragraphs have changed since 1814. Between 1908 and 2008, no less than 583 amendments were tabled to the Constitution, 86 of which were adopted. There are at least two ways to approach a revision — or revisions, if you like — of the Constitution. You can, as the proposers suggest, carry out a complete audit, or you can follow the line that Stortinget has now followed, namely to get into parts of the Constitution. I believe that we need to constantly assess our constitution in relation to today's challenges, but that does not necessarily mean that we are opening up to replace all the provisions and principles on which the mariners – they were men – based their constitution. A full constitutional audit does not necessarily give us a better constitution. On the other hand, there is a danger that it will be marked by many details and compromises. Then the Basic Law loses lightly its soul and its meaning as a guide and a normmaker. The creation of the Human Rights Committee under the direction of Inge Wohnung is an example of drawing out individual themes from the Basic Law, and in the Left's draft document, it is also one of the areas mentioned by the proposers, but it is thus preserved through work in a selection here in the house at the moment. The decision on the closure of nobility and laying, the changes in the national legal system and the incorporation of the church settlement into the Constitution, which is set out later in this enlargement period, are other examples of the Storting's willingness to make important adjustments to the Constitution to bring it more into line with today's reality and the challenges that tomorrow also brings. The proposers originally wanted in their document to ask the government to investigate the changes to the Constitution, as I mentioned here at the outset. So, essentially, questions about constitutional changes have been considered to be the responsibility of the Storting. A completely different question is whether a system is in place that adequately ensures the technical quality assurance of the amendments that are sometimes adopted. It's a question that the Presidency has to consider at all times. With this, I recommend the majority position of the committee.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-12.ana.ud2204e2105	The budget shall continue to examine matters on agenda No. Seventy-four.
ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-12.ana.ud2204e2119	Government purchases goods and services for nearly 400 billion. kr annually. The state alone stood for over 170 billion dollars. kr in 2009. Infringement of the rules on government acquisitions has been a repetition of the National Audit Office’s reporting on state activities. Errors and defects have been mentioned several times. The situation has improved somewhat in some areas, but there is still considerable potential for improvement. Non-compliance with procurement law may result in higher prices and lower quality of the goods and services purchased by government. It also increases the risk of corruption and of maladministration. It is therefore positive that the Riksrevision focuses on the theme. A Joint Committee has noted that the Ministry of the National Audit Office has expressed its broad focus on better compliance with the legislation. This, of course, is good. And that's enough to take hold of. 1 196 regulatory infringements have been detected in the 616 cases where non-compliance with the procurement rules has been found in the period 2006-2009. The most frequent remaining breach of legislation is lack of competition. This applies to more than half of all matters. Often this is due to the continuation of expired framework agreements, where new tenders should have been announced. Another remaining breach of legislation is a lack of or lack of record keeping. This applies in 45 pst. of the cases. Other regulatory breaches detected are lack of traceability, lack of HMS certificate or tax certificate, lack of written contract, incorrect selection of tendering procedures, lack of competitive grounds, failure of publication and breach of the rules on stability. Why, then, is it that the rules are not followed? This is a complex issue, where it is difficult to explain a single reason. The report sets out eleven possible root causes based on surveys of the establishments and departments concerned. Lack of legislative competence is the reason stated by most establishments. Another reason mentioned is a lack of order skills, i.e.: knowledge of the needs of the establishment and the potential suppliers. Often purchasers believe that there is only one current supplier and address him directly when goods and services are to be acquired. Lack of regulatory competence and ordering competence are often due to the low level of organisation of procurement. This has been discussed in the Committee's comments and the Committee refers to Innst. S. No 348 for 2008-2009, cf. St.meld. No 36 for 2008-2009, where measures to improve the situation are also outlined. Increased leadership anchoring, improved internal control, increased competence and increased use of electronic solutions shall contribute to better procurement. The Committee also points out that the Ministry, following recommendations made in NOU 2010:2 Enforcement of public acquisitions, is in the process of designing new rules for the enforcement of public procurement. The Directorate for Administration and ICT – Diffi – has a central place in making public enterprises focus on improvements in the procurement sector. Among other things, Divi operates doffin.no and publishes newsletters with relevant information in the subject field. The target group is all those involved in public acquisitions, and in particular those directly or indirectly responsible for purchases in municipalities and counties. Digi is responsible for the fact that government and other public enterprises have an offer of acquisition price. This is an urgent and useful measure to remedy the situation. The knowledge portal, www.ankver.no, and Digit's work on further developing it with information, organisation and tools is also something that will strengthen this field. Difi publishes a newsletter in the field, as I said. The National Audit Office's report is mentioned in Newsletter No. 9/2011. The newsletter also mentions the digital signature scheme for public procurement in Europe. By signing electronically, purchases are faster and with greater certainty as to who has made the offer and that what has been delivered has not changed along the way. At the same time, the threshold is lowered to obtain cross-border offers. According to the Director of Diffi, this gives better competition to supply to the public sector. The majority of the Committee, the members of the Progress Party, the Right, the Christian People's Party and the Left, also point to a number of measures that can be considered to strengthen the field, such as a separate ‘buying State Council’, increasing the focus on small businesses and voluntary organisations in public procurement, and revising the procedure for the prioritisation of operators. The Committee also points out that the NHO and KS have launched a national supplier development programme. In this context, they have published a brochure showing how open dialogue between customers and suppliers at the early stage of acquisition contributes to new products, new technologies, better solutions and added value for all parties involved. The Committee considers that the size of public tenders should be highlighted. There is a well-known problem that many contracting authorities are struggling with the lack of participants in the competition. When only a few suppliers are involved, competition can be significantly reduced. Diffi refers to a Swedish survey that shows that many small enterprises never participate in public tendering because the contracts published are too large for them. By providing better facilities, there is a high potential for more competitors, greater competition and better public procurement. The majority of the Committee, the members of the Advancement Party, the Right, the Christian People's Party and the Left, point out that for example: can be resolved by placing framework contracts or other major contracts on smaller parts, geographical or product contracts. In that case, smaller enterprises may submit offers to those parts where they have their expertise or geographical impact. Large framework contract competitions should only be conducted where one can achieve a better end result by an overall call than by the publication of the contract in less separate competitions. The complaint subject of public acquisitions, KOFA, deals with breaches of the procurement rules. The development there is ominous. The number of complaints has been significantly increased by the CODA. Although the staff has been increased, the number of cases has almost doubled from 2009 to 2010. The average duration of proceedings is now around six months. This is twice the target set four years ago. But not all illegal direct acquisitions come at KOPA's table. The subject of the complaint does not have the power to investigate cases where there is a suspicion that a contracting entity has failed to publish an acquisition. Complaints are subject to complaints from suppliers, organisations, politicians and others. The Committee welcomes the cooperation between KOFA and the Competition Authority in disseminating knowledge to contracting authorities on procurement law and on rules contained in the Competition Act on illegal tendering. All departments have mentioned acquisitions in the governance dialogue with the underlying activities. The leadership is largely general, with few specific measures affecting each activity. It must be assumed that, in its future governance dialogue, the departments focus more on compliance with the rules and specify, where appropriate, concrete measures to be taken. The Committee has also noted that the Riksrevision points out that the Ministry of Renewal, Administrative and Church Affairs should work more closely on measures to prevent breaches of procurement law. The Committee agrees with this call for the Ministry to be kept informed of the developments. The National Audit Office states that the Ministry should continue its work on measures to ensure sound administrative practices in the field of procurement. The Committee asks the Ministry to follow this call and to continue its work on the guidance, organisation and increased use of electronic commerce, and not least the building of competence in the field. The Progress Party, the Right, the Christian People's Party and the Left also ask for an assessment of the need for simplification of today's legislation, and ask the government to come back to the Stortinget with proposals for amendments.

That's what the government takes seriously. The National Audit Office’s study makes a useful contribution to improving compliance with the rules. The most common error in the cases now examined is that goods and services have been acquired without competitive performance. However, the study also reveals other types of errors, such as the lack of protocols to document the acquisition process, the lack of other types of evidence, the lack of traceability between contracts concluded and the accounting system, and the incorrect use of framework agreements. The study shows that the causes of these errors are complex. Lack of competence, as mentioned above, is the main cause. The Riksrevision study thus largely confirms what was presented in St.meld. No 36 for 2008-2009, Good procurement. Here enterprises can also find guidance on internal control, management anchoring and the establishment of acquisition strategies. Another important measure to ensure good procurement is the increased use of electronic solutions. The government therefore has a five-year effort to increase the use of e-commerce in public acquisitions. E-commerce offers gains in the form of simpler, safer and more efficient public acquisitions. E-commerce also facilitates better compliance with the legislation, since standardisation of procedures and materials obliges purchasers to comply with the rules. In addition, it will be easier to control and track the acquisition process. Surveys by the National Audit Office also point to a lack of data on government acquisitions. This work will necessarily take some time, but it has a high priority and will continue in 2011. Public procurement allows enterprises to be perfected against international markets, and it is clear that acceptance from domestic public procurement organisations will often be a very good reference when it comes to world affairs. Health, care and care are new areas where more and more actors define as interesting, and where the public also has a great share of responsibility for drawing with private suppliers and their ideas and solutions. There should be a high potential for innovation and supplier development, and not least the recent Interaction Reform will lead to a great need to look at old problems with new eyes to find the new solutions, for the benefit of Norwegian business.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-12.ana.ud2204e2306	CO2 cleaning in Mongstad has a long history, and you can actually drag it back to the Bondevik government, even though today's government likes to take all the credit. In 2004 the Bondevik government promoted St.meld. No 47, on the innovation business of environmentally friendly gas power technologies, etc. The Mongstad project, on the other hand, was born in 2006, as a necessary compromise in the government to realise the CHP plant and upgrade the refinery. The story has then been turbulent — ever-new delays, disagreements about information flows, which led to the 2010 mistrust proposal, and an ever-increasing cost growth. This makes that especially in the opposition we have very watchful eyes in this matter. Likewise today, we see a unanimous growth that wants the project to be realised and that supports the adjustments in the project around the test centre in Mongstad. There is evidence of a long-distance opposition, but there is also evidence of a position that has spoken well with the opposition in this case. For many, including those who are signed, the knit is stretched as far as it can go without rushing into patience in this matter. That being said, much indicates that the project is now on the track it should have been at an early stage. I hope we avoid relapses. The project has now been clearly divided into two clear parts. First, build the test centre – and give it time for testing. It gives the project a central role in the attempt to develop commercial clean-up technologies. This clarification is important, because in the big debate in May 2009, the signal from the government parties was that full scale investment decisions should be taken regardless of whether the test centre was finished. This line has the government decayed, and that's good. Next, a full-scale clean-up investment decision is to be made in Mongstad. The decision has been postponed until no later than 2016, as was the case in the Official Journal of Bergen, which in May 2009 predicted that the lunar landing would take place in 2018 or later, quite foresight. But already it looks like you were actually optimistic. With regard to the full-scale clean-up point on Monstad, it is likely that the layers in this setting also separate, where some parties still give the impression of wanting to invest in Mongstad almost costing what it costs, while among other things. The Progress Party and the Right review the resource usage here for alternative projects. The Committee believes that the course that has now been set contributes to making the Mongstad test centre more interesting for research environments and industrial actors. The Committee also stresses that the government has a responsibility to ensure a broad involvement in the research communities. The Committee is set up to meet the need to involve several technologies in the test centre. This is particularly important when the government doubts whether the amine technology is suitable for large-scale CO2 cleaning. We point out that, for example: Siemens is warning that they want to go into the test centre at Mongstad. We hope that the government will handle this in a good way. Gassnova is set to do a screening work for potential CO2 clean-up projects beyond Mongstad. I would like the Council of State to specify a little what he has thought about this. Is it a three-year investigation programme to go to a look at the test facility, or is it an ongoing work that opens the way for the decision to be taken before? Int. S. No 205 for 2006-2007, an overall Energy Committee stated that the government should ‘facilitate further CO2 management projects’. For some of us, it is close to pointing to both Hammerfest Energie and Sargas’ project at Melkøya and at Industrikraft Møre and Siemens’ project in Møre and Romsdal. An overall opposition will allow industrial actors outside Mongstad to implement projects with full-scale CO2 cleaning faster and to obtain the necessary clarifications on government frameworks. So it's not enough that Gassnova investigates it, even though it's a good step in the right direction. An overall committee stresses that health, environment and safety must be at the root of the Mongstad test centre, and we point in particular to the work that is now being done to identify the potential cancer hazard of the use of amines. I would like to remind you that the Committee also mentioned this in 2009. So this isn't a new theme, as you can get the impression through the media. Successful development of CO2 clean technologies depends on a significant reduction in CO2 capture costs and a good subsequent management of CO2. This is important to clarify if we're going to succeed, because it's just developing full-scale technology that gets so expensive that nobody wants it, hasn't given it the breakthrough you need. But the government has also understood that. The Committee pointed out in 2009 that efforts should be made to make CO2 a resource rather than waste. I hope that the government will continue the work to a greater extent than today, and that they will look more into the use of CO2 for oil well injection to increase the extraction there.
ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-12.ana.ud2204e2369	One of the greatest challenges facing the world is climate change. If we're going to reach the two degrees goal, we're going to need a big shift in the way we produce and use energy. Large point emissions account for a substantial part of the emissions of CO2 internationally. In Norway, the 10 maximum point emissions are 20 pst. of the total emission. The clean-up of CO2 emissions from point sources will play an important role in addressing climate challenges both in Norway and internationally. The International Energy Agency, the IEA, some time ago said that if we're going to achieve our climate goals, we need a technological revolution. We need new technologies for renewable energy production, we need good solutions for energy saving, and we need technologies that can capture and store CO2. I am pleased that Norway has taken a leading role internationally in the development of capture and storage technologies. Analyses of what the future energy system will look like show that capture and storage technologies are central to achieving our objectives. There is a large amount of coal-fired power plants in operation today, and new ones are being built. For this very reason, it is extremely important to develop technologies that can capture and store their emissions. The announcement of the full-scale capture and storage of CO2 shows that government parties have set an ambitious course to contribute to the development, testing and commercialisation of such a technology. I think it is very gratifying to see that there is also such broad consensus on the Stortinge on the perspectives that the message draws up. The capture and storage of CO2 on the scale we're talking about here is ambitious. It's necessary to achieve the climate goals, it's new work, and we have to go up the path ourselves. The test centre at Mongstad is being developed according to the plan. When it's done, it's going to be the first thing in its kind in the world, and there's no other test centers that test so many technologies and that can vary CO2 in the flue gases in the order of magnitude that you do at the test centre. I am very pleased that the government has stressed that the partners in the test centre are actors who all have an industrial need for the technology being tested. This gives the project an important anchoring. Through the CLIMIT research programme and the CCS research centre and several other activities, we contribute to the development, testing and commercialisation of CO2 capture and storage technologies. The full scale capture and storage of CO2 is the objective of this work. We have to facilitate the capture and storage of CO2 both in new power plants and in so-called hot power plants, as we do in Mongstad. The existing point emissions in the world, and in Norway, are formidable. The work on full-scale CO2 cleaning in Mongstad is very important. Early on it was chosen to use amine-based technology, because it was the technology that was perceived as clearly the most mature. Now that we have received the information we have about the potential health hazards of amine technology, I am very pleased that we are taking the time to clarify any health problems, and that a technology qualification cycle is now taking place in Mongstad with several technologies – before the investment decision is made. The new cycle in Mongstad means that it is used up to three years for technology qualification and then up to two years for design. This is a common industrial process for working with immature technology. However, I would like to stress that if the question of health risk is resolved before three years have elapsed, the question of technology choice will be re-examined immediately. In addition to the full-scale work in Mongstad, it is important that Gassnova has been commissioned to investigate any other full-scale CO2 management projects in Norway. Together with the efforts of CLIMIT and international efforts, the testing centre at Mongstad and full-scale activity at Mongstad, this provides a comprehensive and offensive policy for the development of this important technology, from idea to production. Finally, I would like to thank the case rapporteur, the representative of Ketil Solvik-Olsen, for good conversations and constructive work that have made us basically – I was saying – come together.
ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-12.ana.ud2204e2438	There is a broad consensus that Norway should contribute to the development of a cost-effective CO2 capture and storage technology. That's why Stortinget has accepted the exceedances at the technology center in Mongstad, and the total bill, which now seems to be 6.5 billion. kr. However, a high price requires good use. It must be an ambition that the technology centre can play a significant role in the development of different capture and storage technologies for CO2 for decades to come and that the utility value is not limited to testing and further developing the two technologies that are now using the technology centre’s facilities. It is therefore positive that several international companies have chosen to purchase on the ownership side of the technology centre. There is a need to ensure a broad involvement in the research environment in Norway and internationally, in order to develop a strong research and business cluster around the Mongstad environment. There is also a broad consensus in this room that good health, environmental and safety standards must underpin the capture of CO2. The ambition must be to solve the challenges of using amines in the purification process. The right has noticed that Aker Clean Carbon believes the challenges are affordable, but we also notice that Statoil is of a different opinion. Research on health hazards associated with the use of amines must be continued and intensified. It doesn't help much to solve a problem by creating a new one. In this context, it is interesting if either Italy and/or the United Kingdom choose to use amine technologies in order to take investment decisions on full scale sewage treatment plants in the third quarter of 2011. If the schedule is maintained, both of these plants will be in operation before the decision on full-scale sewage treatment plants is taken in Mongstad, a decision by the government is announced that it will come in 2016. The revised schedule at Mongstad is likely to provide a significant transfer of experience from the construction and operation of other retrofit facilities. The Government also warns that it will facilitate the assessment of several actors and technologies in full-scale CO2 management. We see that as a correct and clear line break from the government. However, it is even more important that the government opens up to demonstrate CO2 clean technologies elsewhere than in Mongstad, which it has so far argued strongly against. The Prime Minister, former Prime Minister Terje Riis-Johansen, the Minister for the Environment and other prominent government representatives have all excluded support for projects other than the full-scale cleaning of Mongstad. Maybe this reverse operation is based on a recognition that it wasn't Apollo 1 that landed on the moon, but Apollo 11, or that it could be popular on the moon if we were to wait until Mongstad light. Anyway, it's positive that the government has turned around. This means that it is already necessary to send signals to actors such as Industrikraft Møre that the government can be willing to provide support for cleaning costs in addition to the quota price if Industrikraft Møre realises a new gas power plant. A new gas power plant with day-to-day clean-up represents a technology other than retrofitting in Mongstad, but building the first full-scale capture plant for CO2 from a gas plant in the world is itself a significant technology development. Such a sewage treatment plant should be built in accordance with an open tendering procedure, whereby the liability and risk of taxpayers is clearly defined. Siemens has estimated the price of cleaning a new gas power plant for about 2 billion. kr – and then built with a clean-up technology that has none of the health challenges associated with amine technology. There are two-thirds of the amount that the government estimates that the pre-projecting of full-scale clean-up at Mongstad will represent, i.e. the costs incurred before the Stortinget has decided whether or not full-scale clean-up is to be built on Mongstad. The government estimates the cost of full-scale clean-up at Mongstad to between 20 and 25 billion. kr. It's a very high number. The price must be reduced if the technology is to be of interest to more than the world's richest country. And that must be the overall goal of the entire project. The price must be reduced if Stortinget is to be able to defend the project from a climate perspective. The alternate climate gain of 25 billion kr will be significant. The price must be reduced if the project is to motivate other countries to invest in CCS. It is worth noting that other countries budget far lower than the government of Mongstad. The UK Government estimates, for example: to clean up 2 million. tonnes of CO2 from the coal-fired plant Longannet will cost 9 billion. kr including transport and storage. Investment decisions are long to be made in Mongstad. In the meantime, the government should give priority to achieving the costs of full scale clean-up, solving and facilitating the transport and storage of CO2 from multiple point emissions and contributing to the realisation of a new gas power plant with day one clean-up at a suitable location in Norway. The ambition to clean Mongstad must be firm, but the price must be down. Hopefully, the answer will be in the technology center we're now using over 6 billion. kr on.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-12.ana.ud2204e2562	I will begin by addressing first of all the proposals that the Christian People's Party has promoted in this matter. Full-scale cleaning would be in place by the end of 2014 and the investment decision would be taken in 2012. The time limits were set as conditions in the emissions permit granted by the Ministry of the Environment. As part of the government compromise, the emission permit for the Mongstad gas power plant was given directly by the Ministry of the Environment and not by the SFT, as usual practice is. It is therefore inconsequential to see that the same emissions permit is now being amended continuously by the Minister for the Environment of SV as Statoil asks for new releases. All time requirements of the emissions permit have now been removed. In its answer to the questions put by the undersigned, the Ministry of the Environment has transmitted the revised terms of the emissions permit from December 2010. The revised emissions permit replaced the original investment decision requirements by 2012 and the full scale clean-up by 2014 with the following wording: «Full scale CO2 management from the cogeneration unit shall be established as soon as possible within the framework of normal industrial practice» We have to say this is far from a moon landing – this is normal industrial practice. Therefore, today’s Christian People’s Party promotes proposals that the implementation agreement should be renegotiated with a new deadline for full scale clean-up to be in place and with an incentive structure that contributes to the implementation of the project at time and cost. The government raises health risks through the use of amine technologies when they now justify the need for further postponement of the project. The Christian People's Party shares the concern for potential cancer. We're not prepared to gamble with people's lives and health to realize the government's foremost achievement project. In fact, it was the Christian People's Party, with the representative Laila Davøy, who first raised this question in Stortinget on written questions in March 2009. She asked for a guarantee that the government would not go into a solution that would go at the expense of life and health to the inhabitants of North Hordland. The undersigned took the matter further in the inner. S. No 206 for 2008-2009. In fact, the government chose to continue running on the same track for two years after these concerns were raised. If the government had chosen to take these concerns seriously in 2009, and opened up to alternative technologies at that time, we would today have been two years longer in the loop. Now we're back to start again. The government announces five new years of technology qualification, and the leap we had in 2006 is now lost. The proposition also identifies a certain risk of further deferrals beyond that.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-12.ana.ud2204e2611	Report. Paragraph 9 provides a broad review of national and international CO2 management. The work at Mongstad is central to government policy and forms the main part of the communication. In Mongstad, we're doing a job that no one's ever done before. The first step in Mongstad's work is the construction of the technology center. Planning and construction have already provided valuable knowledge. Testing starting next year will provide additional knowledge of CO2 capture. This will also include knowledge of amine emissions and potential health and environmental impacts. We believe and hope that there won't be negative health and environmental impacts of amine emissions to air, but we don't know. We're dependent on being safe about this. Research in recent years has acquired knowledge of the release and formation of amine products. Some of that knowledge is good news, but there is also a need for even more knowledge. We are therefore making a significant effort to ensure knowledge so that amine technology can be put into use within a safe environment and health. The full-scale plant in Mongstad is both a technological new crime and a gigantic industrial project. When we now open up to include more capture technologies than amines in the qualification process, we make adjustments to the project model at the same time. The qualification of capture technology shall take place prior to the design of the installation itself. It makes the project more robust. The technology qualification is estimated to last up to three years and the design phase to last around two years. This suggests that, with today’s information, we can provide an overall decision-making basis for the Stortinget by 2016. If a capture technology becomes sufficiently qualified in the past, this could contribute to faster progress. Efforts are being made to establish a solution for the transport and storage of CO2 to be in place at the start of the full-scale capture in Mongstad. The agreement is a follow-up to the 2006 Implementation Agreement and regulates the rights and obligations of the State and Statoil in the planning phase for full scale CO2 management in Mongstad. The agreement describes the main features of the development and planning work that must be carried out in order to decide on the construction and operation of the fishing facility. The agreement is public and the main points of the agreement are referred to in the main notice. I would also like to explain the content of the agreement in the revised national budget. It is the joint objective of the parties that this agreement be concluded by the end of 2013. The OED is in the process of drawing up a mandate for this work. ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-12.ana.ud2204e2842	Full-scale cleaning of the Mongstad thermal power plant is a boost for unparalleled industrial research in Norway. This is a project that I've been following very closely through the different phases and under different governments. When I was rapporteur in the host municipality, this was a large part of my working day. Progress towards solutions for technology development and security of existing industries was first achieved with Stoltenberg I. The Mongstad refinery has over time been Norway’s largest CO2 emission point. The refinery, before the thermal power plant was built, was partly based on technology developed in the early 1970s. A refinery uses a lot of energy in the production, a lot in the form of heat. Mongstad produced the heat in oil-fired boilers. Gas power plants producing electricity have a relatively cosy ‘waste product’, and it is heat – heat-smooth. In the conversion of Mongstad, the old oil-fired boilers were replaced by heat from the energy plant. The energy plant supplies energy to the refinery, both in the form of electricity and heat, and it supplies electricity to Do and into the local network. The energy plant is now in operation to deliver flue gas to the TCM. The flue gas may be of different quality, with high CO2 content and low CO2 content. It makes it very suitable for research. The project is carried out in what is described as a hot installation — it is in operation. This presents specific challenges that need to be addressed in the course of the process. In order to achieve the overall CO2 reduction targets globally, it is essential to find technologies that can be used in existing installations. The IEA estimates are in need of approx. 3000 full scale CO2 treatment plants by 2050. Many of these necessarily need to be built on existing coal and gas power plants. This is why solutions need to be developed that can easily be built into power plants in operation. The right-hand side now wants to consider moving the full-scale cleaning according to the no cure – no pay principle and the cheapest solution. They argue that it will be cheaper to do so in new installations. Yes, much as possible, but if we do, we lose important knowledge of how this can be solved for plants that are in operation, and it is those that need to be cleaned. The right-hand output of moving the full-scale project breaks down the competence environment that is now being built around the TCM, where research and industrial construction are not only organisational but actually physically co-located for once. Locally, I would like to point out that agreements on the use of CO2 for industrial development have already been drawn up, and that is to convert CO2 from waste and an industrial resource problem. If we move the full-scale clean-up, Norway will lose the positive ring-effects. There is therefore a full scale cleaning project to be carried out in Mongstad. That's when you spend the big money on the big challenges, where it's the most daunting.
