ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-12.ana.ud2204e3064	The last two winters we have experienced a much more difficult supply situation than normal. According to Statnet, low rainfall, problems with nuclear power plants in Sweden and high power consumption have been the main causes of strenuous power supply and high electricity prices. Much more than usual, we have experienced how vulnerable our water-based power supply is. As long as the hydropower is the dominant part of Norwegian power generation, we must accept that we will periodically experience great variations between the availability and consumption of power, as the Council of State correctly writes in its letter to the Committee. He is also quite right that it is decisive that we have a framework that enables us to deal with these types of short-term power situations without causing significant consequences for consumers of power. Right is therefore pleased that the government has finally decided to send an energy message to the Stortinget. The last was the then state council Einar Steensnæs that presented to the Stortinget, in December 2003. It's a long way from a scandal that it's taken so long. Statnet suggests that this winter’s magazine filling was even lower than it was in 2003, in addition to the fact that current consumption is now much higher. However, when the situation is no longer dramatic today, it comes about that we have a better relationship with the rest of the world and thus greater possibilities for importing power, as well as a gas power plant in Kårstø. Against that background, it is a great paradox that today we have an energy minister who will limit the development of the transfer capacity to the rest of Europe. The oil and energy minister will thus make us even more dependent on rain and wind here in the country. He also represents a party that, in its time, went out of government because of its resistance to the gas power plant at Kårstø. While the then State Council Steensnæs presented a big message on security of supply, today's oil and energy ministers have signalled a policy that will weaken our security of supply. The right is fundamentally inconsistent with the Minister of Oil and Energy on this issue. We think we need more, not fewer foreign lines. It will strengthen our security of supply, because then we can import power produced with other energy sources when it's dry and cold here in the country, and there's little water in the warehouse, as we've done the last two winters. It goes without saying that there are limits to how long we can take off the reserves in the reservoir. It is right that Norway has been engaged in the liberalisation of the power market that the energy laws brought with it. The fact that we have an open market in the Nordic region, as well as a power exchange with the rest of Europe, makes us less vulnerable, because we have access to a larger market and more differentiated production. At the same time, our power producers need a market to export to in periods of surplus power. The representative proposal from the Christian People's Party contains many good points to which the Right will join. The right agrees on the need to strengthen the domestic transmission link as well as to strengthen Nordic cooperation through the establishment of new transmission lines to the rest of the world. We are also some in the effort to produce new renewable energy in Norway through a green certificate. We are quite a few in that the authorities have to say yes to the construction of a new gas power plant in central Norway with CO2 handling, where the State covers the extra costs of capture of CO2 in addition to the quota price and takes responsibility for the transport and storage of CO2. The right also fully agrees that there is a need for significant efforts to improve energy efficiency, and that as soon as practicable, advanced measuring systems should be introduced by electricity customers. However, the right does not agree with all of the proposals. It shall apply, inter alia, to: to introduce a parallel to KILE costs if a power company is unable to deliver electricity. It will be a retreat when it comes to energy laws. It cannot simply transfer the principle of KILE costs from the network to power generation. Unlike power generation, which is part of a market with several competing operators, the network operators are in a monopolistic situation. It is therefore not right to compare production and network in this way, and in the view of the Right, it would be negative for the power customers. In order to prevent the emptying of the warehouse, with very high electricity prices as a consequence, the transfer link to the rest of the world should be strengthened so that more power can be imported when Norway is in a deficit situation and exported power in an excess situation. Power generation in Norway should also be strengthened, inter alia: by means of a green certificate and a shorter procedure for concession requests for power generation and transmission systems. This must also be facilitated in the new energy message promised once in the future.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-12.ana.ud2204e3183	Like Solvik-Olsen, it strikes me that I've been on this before. It also struck me that we are discussing the snow that fell last year, both literally and metaphysically. But I look forward to an important debate on an important issue with Stortinget. The security of supply for electricity is about our basic ability to cover the consumption of electricity over time, i.e. evolution of production, consumption and transmission capacity. For the Norwegian hydropower system, this means that we need a power supply that is robust enough to withstand the wide variations we experience in weather conditions from year to year, in this case several years in a row. At the same time, security of supply depends on the organisation of power supplies in a way that enables us to deal with precipitation failures when such situations occur. In order to ensure a lasting security of supply of power at national and regional level, we need a long-term commitment to both: network development new power generation energy efficiency improvement Today, we see that several regions face challenges with sufficient production and transmission capacity. Hydropower has many advantages, but a challenge is that production varies considerably over seasons and years. In order to compensate for these variations, relations with the rest of the world are important. The countries around us have a broader and different composition of power generation than Norway has. We're also going to want to discuss web policy with Stortinget. This will be an important contribution, but I also want to say that we know many of the variables, and that we therefore deliver policies on all the important challenges on an ongoing basis.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-12.ana.ud2204e3451	Norway is today the only country in Europe with no clear energy efficiency improvement targets. There is considerable political focus on energy production, but too little on energy consumption. However, as important as investing in energy-producing buildings, energy efficiency measures are to be introduced into existing building masses. In 2009, Sintef Construction Research estimated that the energy saving potential in the construction sector alone amounted to 12 TWh in the period 2010-2020. It corresponds to the energy consumption of 600 000 dwellings. The Arnstad Committee, which presented its report last summer, has estimated an energy saving potential similar to that of SINTEF. In other words, there is a lot of energy to get from saving energy – and the cheapest and most environmentally friendly energy is as well-known as not being used. Although most energy efficiency measures are self-financing over time, there are very many measures that are still not triggered. The initial investment represents a threshold that many do not have the opportunity to cross. It is therefore necessary to facilitate energy efficiency improvement through public instruments such as: tax incentives for energy saving measures in own household, rights-based direct support in relation to the Oslo model, or white certificates. So far, the government has not decided for any of these measures. That's not good enough. No one can do anything, but everyone can do something. The state should have good assumptions for doing very much. Unfortunately, the experience with the new government quarter, R6, shows that it is a long way from being a model for energy savings. At the same time, it is important to look at costs and objectives in the context of the solutions chosen and to take into account what is most functional for individual constructions. Last year, the low-energy programme confirmed that energy use in buildings can be halved by 2040. The municipal minister has this as an ambition, and progress is already faster than assumed for just a few years. Today, the Norwegian building mass uses a total of 80 TWh energy. To halve this figure, measures must be taken in all types of buildings, both new and old residential buildings and industrial buildings. The committee proposed that all new buildings from 2015 should be passive houses. The EU's recently updated building directive requires new construction from 2020 onwards to be a so-called zero-energy building, in other words, built with particularly low energy needs, which also produces energy itself. Passive house is a step on the road. Active house is next. Correctly, the CO2 emissions from the Norwegian building mass do not exceed 3 pst. of the total emissions, against approx. 35 pst. in Europe. This is because we're essentially using current based on hydropower. But if we release electricity from buildings, this could replace the use of other pollutants. As the proposers are aware, there are many possible ways to speed up the construction of energy-producing buildings. Both instrumentation, procedural efficiency and energy labels are important tools. In addition, the State must set a good example. That's why the government has already done a lot of work to support development. There is already room in Enova for support for passive houses and individual components that can help lift the passive houses to become a plus house. At the same time, we see that the market is currently small. The NVE has granted exemptions from trading concession requirements for consumer customers, which will deliver electricity on the network in profit periods. When we learn more about how the Plus Houses Code works, we will consider further regulatory simplifications. The centre party is impatient in getting valuable power released from the building stock and instead using it to replace fossil energy in, for example, the transport sector and industry. The automatic meter reading system, which was much discussed in the previous case, could also make an important contribution to this.

Such houses are built, among others. in Germany and Austria. The EU Parliament has proposed that all new buildings from 2019 should produce more energy than the consumers, as was also the case with the representative Olsen. Norway is among the countries where energy consumption in buildings is highest. As seen, Norway is also one of the countries most benefiting from energy efficiency measures in the construction sector. A prerequisite for Norwegian plus houses is that the building body is as energy efficient as possible. With good insulation, high density and intelligent ventilation solutions, it is possible to reduce heating requirements to a minimum. In addition, heat recovery solutions for ventilation air and gray water, creative solutions to optimise daylight use and use of only very energy efficient electrical appliances will mean that the building only needs to generate a very limited amount of energy. We in the Left will point out that with a combination of very energy efficient constructions, solar collectors and heat pumps for heating, as well as small wind turbines and solar panels for electricity generation, it will be fully possible to build plus houses in most places along the coast, up to a good distance into North Norway. Also in the interior there will be places that are well suited for plus houses, but here it will call winter could be a challenge. Far north of the country, the low number of sun hours also poses a major challenge. Enova, the government body that provides financial support for energy efficiency improvement and renewable energy production, currently has support systems for the construction of energy-efficient buildings, but they do not support private households. In order to motivate the construction of more energy-efficient houses in all construction categories, this scheme should apply to all types of buildings, just as there are renewable heating support schemes for all the different building categories. Finally, it is fully possible to build a plus house in Norway. At the same time, there are a number of energy efficiency and energy production solutions that can be implemented in the existing building stock and that should be used in new buildings also in places where conditions are not conducive to the construction of plus houses. Given the long life span of buildings, the existing building stock will be with us far into the future. It is then important to implement measures to ensure that energy use in these buildings is as low as possible.

They showed, not least, that independent third party assessments bring new knowledge and provide the basis for more nuanced assessments in these debates. There is a broad consensus that the supply situation in Bergen is unacceptable and needs to be improved. But the investigations showed that there has not been a basis for the crisis awareness presented last year, where it was said that sea cable would be a poor solution for security of supply, and that sea cable made the connection of new small power difficult. It was important to point out for SV's part. The fact that a sea cable represents a significant additional cost and involves its own intervention is, of course, no doubt. The government chose to make a priority and went for the air voltage option, which I think is a pity. There is no doubt that the security of supply of the mountain area now becomes significantly safer, but I think all the conflict that has arisen as a result of this case could have been avoided with better anchoring in the concession processes. Those parts of the Progress Party's proposal that are not polemics, and that is a significant part of the document that is polemics, I have the basic sense of it. SV has had close contact with, among others: The action Preserve Hardanger!, and one of the options we've discussed a lot, is the possibilities for voltage upgrade of existing networks. With a more forward-looking network policy in Norway at a much earlier time – and I would like to remind you here that investments in the network fell to a low level after the Energy Act was adopted early in the 1990s, and reached the zero point at the turn of the millennium – this matter might have been avoided. However, this proposal should not be accepted. There is little economy, little social economy and poor environmental policies to make a region almost permanently dependent on, for example: a reserve gas power plant, and there is reason to recall that the government is working hard to lift Mid-Norway out of a situation where the reserve gas power plants are necessary.
ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-12.ana.ud2204e3775	In the first three months of 2010, according to Statnett, the mountain area experienced more than 1,300 hours when a fault would darken more than 40 pst. by the city. If something had happened in these hours, the consequences for consumers would be huge, immediate and unacceptable. The supply situation of the mountainous region needs to be improved, both in view of current uncertainties and in view of the future growth of the region. As it is now, the mains power supply does not meet the maximum demand for power. On cold days more electricity is used in the area than it is produced. If more than today's main lines towards the area are used, they can be transported alone and there are errors on one of the two main lines currently in existence, consumers need to be disconnected in order to avoid congestion and even more extensive and long-term power cuts. This must be avoided. The infrastructure must ensure sufficient electricity for all when the consumption is at its highest. Better line capacity must therefore be set as soon as possible. We cannot accept the danger that Bergen will go in black. We must contribute to the creation of new power generation in the area. As a result, we also contribute to local economic development. A number of applications for small power plants, with a total potential of around 115 GWh, are now dusting down pending new transmission capacity. Statnet’s calculations show that it will take ten to twelve years before the upgrade has been completed. This assumes that the new power supply is in place first. If this is not the case, it will take even longer since the works can only be carried out in low load periods. If you build in parallel instead, this will create new natural interventions and thus new conflicts. The centre party believes that it will take an unacceptable long time before this option can be implemented. Furthermore, we believe that it is partly based on unrealistic assumptions. Therefore, the Central Party believes that the power line between Sima and Samnanger must be built. This is the solution that will contribute most rapidly to the realisation of new power generation. It is a step on the way to the goal of a smoother price of electricity, a secure power supply and opportunities for economic growth and increased well-being for most people. That's good central party policy.
ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-12.ana.ud2204e3790	Let me first say that it is an important question that is addressed to debate in the Stortinget today. What has been and is most important for the government is to ensure a safe and secure power supply in all parts of the country. That means we have to build more networks, cf. the debates we have had earlier this evening, where I have seen a broad majority at least agree with the idea and the importance of the development of the network. So I think that the representative Serigstad Valen is right when he says that there are many people who have learned a lot about this matter. It is one of the first major line developments that has taken place in Norway for a very long time. My hope and ambition are, of course, that future projects will be able to be implemented with a much lower level of conflict than what has been achieved here. Having said that, I believe that the line we are now building must be one of Norway's best-earned lines – not to say the world. All possible variables and combinations of variables have been taken into account for very thorough assessments. So to the content of the proposal. So here you set the voltage upgrade to the government's point of view of building a new line. Voltage upgrade as an alternative to new transmission was considered during NVE concession treatment. The NVE concluded that, in the interests of security of supply, it would be difficult to take the pipelines for a longer period of time to convert them to 420 kV. During the appeal proceedings, the Ministry of Statenet requested additional information on the voltage upgrade. Based on Statnett’s feedback and notes prepared by the Norwegian Tourist Association, the Ministry re-evaluated the stress upgrade in the review process, after obtaining the NVE’s comments on all material received on this issue. I refer to the department's decision on the complaint in a letter dated 2 July 2010, where the voltage upgrade was reviewed as one of several alternative options for the transfer line from Sima to Samnanger. The Ministry concluded there that it was not an appropriate alternative to upgrade existing lines until a third transmission line entered the area to secure the supply during the installation period. Voltage upgrade is also described by the samples commissioned to assess the sea cable option for Sima-Samninger. A voltage upgrade will extend over 10 to 12 years. However, this assumes that a new power supply is in place first. Without a new power supply, the upgrade will take additional time because the works can only be carried out for short low load periods. New generation of power to the extent and availability required to carry out a voltage upgrade will, in reality, have to be a gas power plant. A gas power plant will not be able to produce during the maximum profit periods, as a result of export restrictions from the region already in force during periods of high tenacity. There are currently very limited opportunities to link new production to the network in the BKK area. In the NVE, notifications and applications for small power plants with a total production of around 115 GWh are pending until new transmission capacity is established. Statnett has further assessed that the Sima-Samnanger line can enable the creation of 1 TWh renewable energy production. In the event of a voltage upgrade, plans for new production must be put on ice until it is completed. The Sima-Samnanger power line is the option which can contribute as quickly as possible to the creation of new power generation in the region, thus contributing to local economic development and value creation. I am of the opinion that there is a basis for saying that it is not a valid option to upgrade existing wires until there is a third transmission line into the area. A new transmission line from Sima to Samnanger was therefore considered necessary in the Ministry’s letter of 1 March. Consequently, the concession decision of 2 July 2010 is final.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-12.ana.ud2204e3845	When I arrived at Stortinget in 2005, the People's reaction to power lines in Hardanger was among the first to make contact with me, and there have been many meetings and many rounds before we stand here today. A large local commitment and a national commitment led the government to decide that we should have an additional study on sea cable in the autumn of 2010. I believe that the security of supply in the BKK area is not good enough today, and that measures need to be taken to ensure that security meets the standards required for the rest of Norway and Europe. Following the submission of its reports by the four government-appointed expert committees on 1 February, we have gained new and improved knowledge in order to conclude on the choice of measures to achieve adequate security of supply in the mountainous region. Samples indicate that current security of supply does not meet the requirement to maintain supply even if a line falls out. The samples say that it is technically possible to choose a sea cable, but that it will be significantly more expensive and time consuming to put in place than air voltage. The government fell on, as we know, to build a new power line in the air between Sima and Samnanger. Lack of networking limits economic growth and job creation. The Council of State stated that it had to be developed in the future, which has been neglected for several years. I believe that we cannot allow the BKK area to become a separate price range, which can lead to that in cold winter days we can experience 40–50 pst. increased electricity price in relation to the current situation and at times a significantly higher price than the rest of the country. In addition to the fact that this uncertainty will create restrictions on economic growth and new jobs, a separate price range will also threaten existing industrial workplaces in the mountainous region – an industry that is already struggling with high electricity prices and the effects of the financial crisis. I would also like to highlight the need for a reliable power supply for a high-growth region. We cannot live with the fact that today's situation poses a risk of decoupling and high prices for mountainers and other hordes. I am pleased that the government has concluded, so the work started to ensure safer energy supply and a more even price of electricity. In the wake of the case, the government will look at the process of concession and construction of power lines in order to get a better anchoring locally. It is good, although it does not help those in Hardanger and others who are disappointed that the sea cable option was not chosen.
